Kronecker Delta Rule for Long Context Linear Attention
Anamaria-Roberta Hartl* 1
Levente Zólyomi* 1,2
Michael List 1
Günter Klambauer 1,2
Sebastian Böck 2
Sepp Hochreiter 1,2
Figure 1: Representation of the memory matrix state revision for mLSTM, Gated DeltaNet, and KronLSTM.
Interference with stored values: mLSTM affects all values, Gated DeltaNet affects those with overlapping keys, and KronLSTM affects only those with overlapping key and value pairs.
1 Introduction
Large language models (LLMs)
predominantly use the Transformer architecture ( Vaswani et al., 2017 ; Touvron et al., 2023a ; Qwen Team, 2023 ; OpenAI, 2025 ) ,
whose softmax attention mechanism
has computational costs that grow quadratically with
context length,
while its key–value cache grows linearly.
Consequently, inference becomes increasingly costly as contexts grow.
To reduce these costs, recent work has explored replacing softmax attention
with linear attention mechanisms ( Katharopoulos et al., 2020 ; Schlag et al., 2021 ) ,
which can be traced back to the idea of fast weight programmers
( Schmidhuber, 1992 ) .
Linear attention compresses the context
into a fixed-size memory matrix that is updated as new tokens arrive,
yielding linear computational complexity and a constant memory footprint.
Recently, recurrent architectures based on associative memories
( Kohonen, 1972 ; Anderson, 1972 ) , including xLSTM
( Beck et al., 2024 ) , as well as related state-space and memory-based
approaches such as Mamba ( Gu and Dao, 2024 ; Dao and Gu, 2024 ; Lahoti et al., 2026 ) ,
DeltaNet ( Yang et al., 2024 ; Yang et al., 2025 ; Kimi Team et al., 2025 ; Hatamizadeh et al., 2026 ) , RWKV-5 and 6 ( Peng et al., 2024 ) ,
and other recent architectures ( Sun et al., 2024 ; Liu et al., 2025 ; Behrouz et al., 2025 ) ,
have emerged as competitive alternatives to Transformers.
Figure 2: Stress test with high learning rates for the same
mLSTM and KronLSTM architectures on language modeling.
Learning becomes unstable for mLSTM
as the gradient norm spikes show, while KronLSTM yields stable learning.
A crucial challenge for linear attention is controlling
memory growth.
If the memory of recurrent
architectures grows without being bounded, its readout can become
arbitrarily large, leading to numerical instabilities,
particularly during learning. The original LSTM
( Hochreiter, 1991 ; Hochreiter and Schmidhuber, 1997 ) uses a bounded squashing function to
control the exposed cell state, while its forget gate regulates the
accumulation of information in memory. RWKV-5 and RWKV-6
( Peng et al., 2024 ) similarly control memory growth
through a forget gate. Mamba architectures
( Gu and Dao, 2024 ; Dao and Gu, 2024 ; Lahoti et al., 2026 ) also restrain
memory growth through a forget mechanism derived from a stable
state-space ordinary differential equation.
However, forget-based memory control becomes increasingly restrictive for
long contexts. To retain information over long time spans, forget gates
must remain close to one; otherwise, information from the
beginning of the context is progressively attenuated. xLSTM
( Beck et al., 2024 ) addresses this limitation by allowing forget gates close to
one and introducing normalizer and stabilizer states to control the scale
of its matrix memory and readout. However, normalization based on ratios
of potentially large quantities can itself lead to numerical difficulties
and learning instabilities, particularly in larger xLSTM networks
( Beck et al., 2025b ) .
mLSTM’s numerical instabilities are illustrated by the stress test in
Figure 2 .
The DeltaNet family ( Yang et al., 2024 ; Yang et al., 2025 ; Kimi Team et al., 2025 ; Hatamizadeh et al., 2026 ) controls memory growth through
Delta Rule updates, whose dynamics converge to a steady-state distribution
with bounded memory ensuring stable learning dynamics.
However, revising a stored value can perturb previously
stored values, leading to unwanted memory interference for long sequences.
We introduce KronLSTM, which, like (Gated) DeltaNet, provides bounded memory
through a delta-style update, but uses the Kronecker Delta Rule.
The Kronecker Delta Rule is less invasive: it modifies the memory only along
a single direction in matrix space, reducing interference with
previously stored associations. This targeted memory revision results
in a less noisy gradient flow over
time.
Our contributions are:
•
(i) Kronecker Delta Rule is introduced as a new update rule for associative matrix memories, which corrects the scalar associations while having minimal interference with previously stored values.
•
(ii) KronLSTM is introduced as a novel linear attention architecture based on the Kronecker Delta Rule equipped with a vector forget gate, a β \beta gate, and a target gate.
•
(iii) Stability properties for the Kronecker Delta Rule are mathematically proved via bounds on the expected memory norm. These bounds ensure stable learning and proper gradient flow.
•
(iv) Experiments empirically validate the strong performance of KronLSTM at long-context language modeling and retrieval tasks. KronLSTM performs strongly across language modeling, recall, and state tracking benchmarks.
2 KronLSTM
In Subsection 2.1 ,
we introduce the Kronecker Delta Rule and analyze its memory dynamics, and
in Subsection 2.2 , the Kronecker Delta Rule is embedded in the KronLSTM sequence-mixer block.
2.1 Kronecker Delta Rule
Linear attention update rules for matrix memory.
Linear attention ( Katharopoulos et al., 2020 ) compresses the context
into a matrix memory 𝑺 \bm{S} that is updated per token and read out per query,
𝑺 t + 1 = 𝑺 t + 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ , 𝒉 t = 𝑺 t ​ 𝒒 t = ∑ i < t 𝒗 i ​ ( 𝒌 i ⊤ ​ 𝒒 t ) , \bm{S}_{t+1}\;=\;\bm{S}_{t}\;+\;\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}\,,\qquad\bm{h}_{t}\;=\;\bm{S}_{t}\bm{q}_{t}\;=\;\sum_{i<t}\bm{v}_{i}\,\big(\bm{k}_{i}^{\top}\bm{q}_{t}\big)\,,
(1)
with 𝑺 t ∈ ℝ d v × d k \bm{S}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}\times d_{k}} , keys 𝒌 t \bm{k}_{t} and queries 𝒒 t \bm{q}_{t} in
ℝ d k \mathbb{R}^{d_{k}} , and values 𝒗 t \bm{v}_{t} in ℝ d v \mathbb{R}^{d_{v}} . Therefore, 𝑺 \bm{S} is an associative
memory ( Kohonen, 1972 ; Anderson, 1972 ) with a Hebbian rule, i.e.,
an outer product update rule.
Modern linear attention architectures like
xLSTM ( Beck et al., 2024 ) ,
Mamba ( Gu and Dao, 2024 ; Dao and Gu, 2024 ; Lahoti et al., 2026 ) ,
RWKV-5 and 6 ( Peng et al., 2024 )
rely on these associative memories equipped with gates and transformations.
The memory growth is controlled by a forget gate, which
stabilizes the memory dynamics.
In contrast, the Delta Rule
( Widrow and Hoff, 1960 ; Schlag et al., 2021 ) corrects the memory with the
difference of the retrieval to the current value when writing into the memory
𝑺 t + 1 = 𝑺 t − β t ​ ( 𝑺 t ​ 𝒌 t − 𝒗 t ) ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ = 𝑺 t ​ ( 𝑰 − β t ​ 𝒌 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ ) + β t ​ 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ , \bm{S}_{t+1}\;=\;\bm{S}_{t}\;-\;\beta_{t}\big(\bm{S}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}-\bm{v}_{t}\big)\,\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}\;=\;\bm{S}_{t}\big(\bm{I}-\beta_{t}\,\bm{k}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}\big)\;+\;\beta_{t}\,\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top},
(2)
with step size β t \beta_{t} . Each step is gradient descent on
1 2 ​ ‖ 𝑺 t ​ 𝒌 t − 𝒗 t ‖ 2 \tfrac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{S}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}-\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert^{2} , and the transition
𝑰 − β t ​ 𝒌 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ \bm{I}-\beta_{t}\bm{k}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top} is a generalized Householder matrix, which admits chunkwise-parallel training
( Yang et al., 2024 ) .
The two rules differ in how an association, once stored, is changed.
The Hebbian write of ( 1 ) is additive, so the memory grows with
the sequence and an earlier value can be buried by later writes but never
revised. The delta rule ( 2 ) revises: it removes the value
currently stored at 𝒌 t \bm{k}_{t} before writing 𝒗 t \bm{v}_{t} . The correction, however,
proceeds along ( 𝑺 t ​ 𝒌 t − 𝒗 t ) ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ (\bm{S}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}-\bm{v}_{t})\bm{k}_{t}^{\top} , a direction determined by
the current memory content, so values stored at keys that overlap with
𝒌 t \bm{k}_{t} are affected as well. We aim at an error-correcting write
that acts solely on the pair ( 𝒌 t , 𝒗 t ) (\bm{k}_{t},\bm{v}_{t}) .
Kronecker Delta Rule.
Let 𝑺 t ∈ ℝ d v × d k \bm{S}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}\times d_{k}} be the memory matrix, 𝒌 t ∈ ℝ d k \bm{k}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}} and
𝒗 t ∈ ℝ d v \bm{v}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}} the key and value at step t t , c t ∈ ℝ c_{t}\in\mathbb{R} a target
and β t \beta_{t} a step size. The scalar 𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑺 t ​ 𝒌 t \bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}_{t}\bm{k}_{t} measures how
strongly the memory associates 𝒌 t \bm{k}_{t} with 𝒗 t \bm{v}_{t} .
The Kronecker Delta Rule corrects this
scalar towards the target along the outer product of the pair as follows
𝑺 t + 1 = 𝑺 t − β t ​ ( 𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑺 t ​ 𝒌 t − c t ) ​ 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ = 𝑺 t − β t ​ ( 𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑺 t ​ 𝒌 t ) ​ 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ ⏟ erase + β t ​ c t ​ 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ ⏟ write . \bm{S}_{t+1}\;=\;\bm{S}_{t}\;-\;\beta_{t}\big(\bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}\,-\,c_{t}\big)\,\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}\;=\;\bm{S}_{t}\;-\;\underbrace{\beta_{t}\big(\bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}\big)\,\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}}_{\text{erase}}\;+\;\underbrace{\beta_{t}\,c_{t}\,\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}}_{\text{write}}\,.
(3)
In place of the d v d_{v} -dimensional residual 𝑺 t ​ 𝒌 t − 𝒗 t \bm{S}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}-\bm{v}_{t} of the delta
rule, a scalar is corrected, and the direction 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ \bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top} of
the correction is determined by the input pair alone without
utilizing the memory content.
A target c t ≡ 1 c_{t}\equiv 1 stores each association with unit strength,
while c t = 0 c_{t}=0 erases it. The target c t c_{t} may depend on the input,
i.e., serves as a gate,
so that what is written becomes input-dependent.
The right-hand side of ( 3 ) separates
the update into an erase and a write term, both scaled by β t \beta_{t} . Choosing
the target c ~ t = c t / β t \tilde{c}_{t}=c_{t}/\beta_{t} removes the step size from the write,
so that β t \beta_{t} alone determines how much of the stored association is
erased and c t c_{t} alone how much is written. KronLSTM uses this form, with both
quantities computed from the input as gates
(Section 2.2 ).
Properties of the Kronecker Delta Rule.
(i) Error correction (see Appendix Property A2
and Property A6 ). An update is a step of gradient descent with step
size β t \beta_{t} on 1 2 ​ ( 𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑺 ​ 𝒌 t − c t ) 2 \tfrac{1}{2}\big(\bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{k}_{t}-c_{t}\big)^{2} .
Re-presenting the same pair gives
𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑺 t + 1 ​ 𝒌 t − c t = ( 𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑺 t ​ 𝒌 t − c t ) ​ ( 1 − β t ​ ‖ 𝒂 t ‖ 2 ) \bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}_{t+1}\bm{k}_{t}-c_{t}=\left(\bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}-c_{t}\right)(1-\beta_{t}\left\lVert\bm{a}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}) , so it
contracts the error iff 0 < β t < 2 / ‖ 𝒂 t ‖ 2 0<\beta_{t}<2/\left\lVert\bm{a}_{t}\right\rVert^{2} , and the Newton step
β t = 1 / ‖ 𝒂 t ‖ 2 \beta_{t}=1/\left\lVert\bm{a}_{t}\right\rVert^{2} meets the target in a single update; for
c t = 0 c_{t}=0 this deletes the association.
(ii) Delta Rule in vectorized form (see Appendix Property A7 ).
With the Kronecker product 𝒂 t = 𝒌 t ⊗ 𝒗 t = vec ⁡ ( 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ ) \bm{a}_{t}=\bm{k}_{t}\otimes\bm{v}_{t}=\vecop(\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top})
and the vectorized memory 𝒔 = vec ⁡ ( 𝑺 ) \bm{s}=\vecop(\bm{S}) , we have
𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑺 ​ 𝒌 t = 𝒂 t ⊤ ​ 𝒔 \bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{k}_{t}=\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\bm{s} and
‖ 𝒂 t ‖ 2 = ‖ 𝒌 t ‖ 2 ​ ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ 2 \left\lVert\bm{a}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}=\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert^{2} , and ( 3 )
becomes
𝒔 t + 1 = ( 𝑰 − β t ​ 𝒂 t ​ 𝒂 t ⊤ ) ​ 𝒔 t + β t ​ c t ​ 𝒂 t , \bm{s}_{t+1}\;=\;\big(\bm{I}-\beta_{t}\,\bm{a}_{t}\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\big)\,\bm{s}_{t}\;+\;\beta_{t}\,c_{t}\,\bm{a}_{t}\,,
(4)
the Widrow–Hoff Delta Rule ( Widrow and Hoff, 1960 ) with input 𝒂 t \bm{a}_{t} and target
c t c_{t} , which gives the rule its name. The transition factor is a generalized
Householder matrix that does not depend on c t c_{t} , so the chunkwise-parallel
formulation of DeltaNet ( Yang et al., 2024 ) applies with 𝒂 t \bm{a}_{t} in
place of 𝒌 t \bm{k}_{t} , the target entering only the driving term. For
β t ​ ‖ 𝒂 t ‖ 2 ∈ ( 1 , 2 ) \beta_{t}\left\lVert\bm{a}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}\in(1,2) the transition factor has a negative eigenvalue, the
idea behind the state-tracking capabilities of Delta Rule models
( Grazzi et al., 2025 ) .
(iii) Interference (see Appendix Property A9 ). For any other stored pair ( 𝒌 i , 𝒗 i ) (\bm{k}_{i},\bm{v}_{i}) , the update
changes the association by
Δ ⁡ ( 𝒗 i ⊤ ​ 𝑺 ​ 𝒌 i ) = − β t ​ ( 𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑺 t ​ 𝒌 t − c t ) ​ ( 𝒗 i ⊤ ​ 𝒗 t ) ​ ( 𝒌 t ⊤ ​ 𝒌 i ) , \Delta\big(\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{k}_{i}\big)\;=\;-\,\beta_{t}\,\big(\bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}-c_{t}\big)\,\big(\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{v}_{t}\big)\big(\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}\bm{k}_{i}\big)\,,
(5)
the product of value overlap and key overlap. Orthogonality in either factor
protects a stored association, whereas the Hebbian and the Delta
rule interference is controlled by key overlap alone.
(iv) Capacity (see Appendix Property A11 ). Each triple ( 𝒌 t , 𝒗 t , c t ) (\bm{k}_{t},\bm{v}_{t},c_{t}) imposes one linear
constraint 𝒂 t ⊤ ​ 𝒔 = c t \bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\bm{s}=c_{t} on the d k ​ d v d_{k}d_{v} entries of 𝑺 \bm{S} , so up to
d k ​ d v d_{k}d_{v} scalar associations can be stored exactly when the features
{ 𝒂 i } \{\bm{a}_{i}\} are linearly independent, against d k d_{k} value vectors for
𝑺 ​ 𝒌 i = 𝒗 i \bm{S}\bm{k}_{i}=\bm{v}_{i} . In particular, a single key can hold several mutually
orthogonal values, which the read-out 𝑺 ​ 𝒌 \bm{S}\bm{k} returns as their sum.
See Appendix A3 for more properties and more details on the above properties.
Convergence, steady state, and memory norm bounds.
We will present expected bounds on the memory norm, which
in turn imply stable memory dynamics that obviate
numerical instabilities.
Memories grow with the length of the context and the number of vectors or associations to store; therefore
long contexts are most relevant when analyzing memory stability.
We model a long context as a stream of i.i.d. triples
( 𝒌 t , 𝒗 t , c t ) ∼ p (\bm{k}_{t},\bm{v}_{t},c_{t})\sim p with fixed step size β \beta and set
𝑨 ¯ := E ⁡ [ 𝒂 ​ 𝒂 ⊤ ] ≻ 𝟎 , 𝒃 ¯ := E ⁡ [ c ​ 𝒂 ] , 𝒔 LS := 𝑨 ¯ − 1 ​ 𝒃 ¯ , ρ := c − 𝒂 ⊤ ​ 𝒔 LS , κ := β ​ max i ​ ‖ 𝒂 i ‖ 2 , \bar{\bm{A}}:=\mathbf{\mathrm{E}}[\bm{a}\bm{a}^{\top}]\succ\bm{0},\quad\bar{\bm{b}}:=\mathbf{\mathrm{E}}[c\,\bm{a}],\quad\bm{s}^{\mathrm{L}\mathrm{S}}:=\bar{\bm{A}}^{-1}\bar{\bm{b}},\quad\rho:=c-\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{s}^{\mathrm{L}\mathrm{S}},\quad\kappa:=\beta\max_{i}\left\lVert\bm{a}_{i}\right\rVert^{2},
(6)
so 𝑺 LS = argmin 𝑺 E ⁡ [ ( 𝒗 ⊤ ​ 𝑺 ​ 𝒌 − c ) 2 ] \bm{S}^{\mathrm{L}\mathrm{S}}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}\,}_{\bm{S}}\mathbf{\mathrm{E}}[(\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{k}-c)^{2}] is the
least-squares memory and ρ \rho the irreducible residual, with
E ⁡ [ ρ ​ 𝒂 ] = 𝟎 \mathbf{\mathrm{E}}[\rho\,\bm{a}]=\bm{0} . Throughout, 0 < β ​ ‖ 𝒂 i ‖ 2 < 2 0<\beta\left\lVert\bm{a}_{i}\right\rVert^{2}<2 , and
q β := 1 − β ⁡ ( 2 − κ ) ​ λ min ​ ( 𝑨 ¯ ) q_{\beta}:=1-\beta(2-\kappa)\lambda_{\min}(\bar{\bm{A}}) .
Theorem 1 (Unbiased mean and steady state) .
The expected iterate obeys the exact recursion
E ⁡ [ 𝐬 t + 1 ] = ( 𝐈 − β ​ 𝐀 ¯ ) ​ E ​ [ 𝐬 t ] + β ​ 𝐛 ¯ \mathbf{\mathrm{E}}[\bm{s}_{t+1}]=(\bm{I}-\beta\bar{\bm{A}})\mathbf{\mathrm{E}}[\bm{s}_{t}]+\beta\bar{\bm{b}} ,
whose unique fixed point is 𝐬 LS \bm{s}^{\mathrm{L}\mathrm{S}} , so
‖ E ⁡ [ 𝐒 t ] − 𝐒 LS ‖ F ≤ r t ​ ‖ 𝐒 0 − 𝐒 LS ‖ F \left\lVert\mathbf{\mathrm{E}}[\bm{S}_{t}]-\bm{S}^{\mathrm{L}\mathrm{S}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}\leq r^{t}\left\lVert\bm{S}_{0}-\bm{S}^{\mathrm{L}\mathrm{S}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}} with
r = max k ⁡ | 1 − β ​ λ k ​ ( 𝐀 ¯ ) | < 1 r=\max_{k}{{\left|1-\beta\lambda_{k}(\bar{\bm{A}})\right|}}<1 . Moreover the
iterates form a time-homogeneous Markov chain on ℝ d k ​ d v \mathbb{R}^{d_{k}d_{v}} whose
kernel contracts on laws with finite second moment; by the standard
theory of Markov chains on general state spaces ( Meyn and Tweedie, 1993 ) it has a
unique stationary distribution π β \pi_{\beta} , the law of 𝐒 t \bm{S}_{t}
converges to it geometrically from any initialization, and the
stationary mean is exactly E π β ​ [ 𝐒 ] = 𝐒 LS \mathbf{\mathrm{E}}_{\pi_{\beta}}[\bm{S}]=\bm{S}^{\mathrm{L}\mathrm{S}} .
Theorem 1 combines Appendix Theorem A6
and Appendix Theorem A7 which have been proven in
Appendix A3 .
Theorem 2 (Memory norm bounds) .
With λ min , λ max \lambda_{\min},\lambda_{\max} the extreme eigenvalues of
𝐀 ¯ \bar{\bm{A}} , the least-squares memory is sandwiched by the
explained target energy,
E ⁡ [ c 2 ] − E ⁡ [ ρ 2 ] λ max ​ ( 𝑨 ¯ ) ≤ ‖ 𝑺 LS ‖ F 2 ≤ E ⁡ [ c 2 ] − E ⁡ [ ρ 2 ] λ min ​ ( 𝑨 ¯ ) , \frac{\mathbf{\mathrm{E}}[c^{2}]-\mathbf{\mathrm{E}}[\rho^{2}]}{\lambda_{\max}(\bar{\bm{A}})}\;\;\leq\;\;\left\lVert\bm{S}^{\mathrm{L}\mathrm{S}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\;\;\leq\;\;\frac{\mathbf{\mathrm{E}}[c^{2}]-\mathbf{\mathrm{E}}[\rho^{2}]}{\lambda_{\min}(\bar{\bm{A}})}\,,
(7)
and all its singular values obey the same upper bound; residual energy
can only shrink it. In the non-expansive regime κ ≤ 1 \kappa\leq 1 , the
steady state and — started at 𝐒 0 = 𝟎 \bm{S}_{0}=\bm{0} — every iterate satisfies
E π β ​ [ ‖ 𝑺 ‖ F 2 ] ≤ E ⁡ [ c 2 ] λ min ​ ( 𝑨 ¯ ) , E ⁡ [ ‖ 𝑺 t ‖ F 2 ] ≤ ( 1 + q β t / 2 ) 2 ​ E ⁡ [ c 2 ] λ min ​ ( 𝑨 ¯ ) ≤ 4 ​ E ​ [ c 2 ] λ min ​ ( 𝑨 ¯ ) , \mathbf{\mathrm{E}}_{\pi_{\beta}}\big[\left\lVert\bm{S}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\big]\;\leq\;\frac{\mathbf{\mathrm{E}}[c^{2}]}{\lambda_{\min}(\bar{\bm{A}})}\,,\qquad\mathbf{\mathrm{E}}\big[\left\lVert\bm{S}_{t}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\big]\;\leq\;\Big(1+q_{\beta}^{\,t/2}\Big)^{2}\frac{\mathbf{\mathrm{E}}[c^{2}]}{\lambda_{\min}(\bar{\bm{A}})}\;\leq\;\frac{4\,\mathbf{\mathrm{E}}[c^{2}]}{\lambda_{\min}(\bar{\bm{A}})}\,,
(8)
the transient factor decays geometrically from 4 4 to 1 1 .
Theorem 2 combines Appendix Theorem A8 ,
Appendix Theorem A9 , and Appendix Theorem A10
which are proven in
Appendix A3 .
From the first step onward, the expected memory of the rule is
bounded by a constant determined by the data distribution alone.
More details and theoretical results are
given in Appendix A3 .
2.2 The KronLSTM architecture
In modern deep learning architectures for sequence modeling,
the matrix update rules are stabilized and enhanced with gating mechanisms.
The mLSTM ( Beck et al., 2024 ) places the Hebbian write ( 1 )
under a sigmoid forget gate and an exponential input gate, and Gated DeltaNet
( Yang et al., 2025 ) decays the memory of ( 2 ) by a scalar
before the correction,
𝑺 t + 1 = f t ​ 𝑺 t + i t ​ 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ , 𝑺 t + 1 = f t ​ 𝑺 t ​ ( 𝑰 − β t ​ 𝒌 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ ) + β t ​ 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ . \bm{S}_{t+1}\;=\;\mathrm{f}_{t}\,\bm{S}_{t}\;+\;\mathrm{i}_{t}\,\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}\,,\qquad\bm{S}_{t+1}\;=\;\mathrm{f}_{t}\,\bm{S}_{t}\,\big(\bm{I}-\beta_{t}\,\bm{k}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}\big)\;+\;\beta_{t}\,\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}\,.
(9)
Kimi Delta Attention ( Kimi Team et al., 2025 ) makes the decay (key) channel-wise, and
Gated DeltaNet-2 ( Hatamizadeh et al., 2026 ) replaces the single β t \beta_{t} by
separate channel-wise erase and write gates. KronLSTM proceeds in the same way: the step
size β t \beta_{t} and the target c t c_{t} of ( 3 ) become gates
computed from the input; a forget gate decays the memory, and the rule is
embedded in the projections, short convolutions, normalizations, and output
gate of a standard sequence-mixer layer. All quantities below are per head,
with key and value dimensions d k d_{k} and d v d_{v} ; we suppress the head index and
write 𝒙 t ∈ ℝ d \bm{x}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d} for the layer input at step t t .
KronLSTM update.
The forget gate is a diagonal matrix
𝑭 t \bm{F}_{t} with diagonal entries in ( 0 , 1 ) (0,1) . It
acts on the key dimension and hence decays the memory from the right,
𝑺 t ​ 𝑭 t \bm{S}_{t}\bm{F}_{t} , retaining each key direction separately.
We use target c ~ t = c t / β t \tilde{c}_{t}=c_{t}/\beta_{t} to decouple erase and write.
The erase term of
( 3 ) is evaluated on the decayed memory 𝑺 t ​ 𝑭 t \bm{S}_{t}\bm{F}_{t} . We obtain
𝑺 t + 1 = 𝑺 t ​ 𝑭 t ⏟ forget − β t ​ ( 𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑺 t ​ 𝑭 t ​ 𝒌 t ) ​ 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ ⏟ erase + c t ​ 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ ⏟ write , \bm{S}_{t+1}\;=\;\underbrace{\bm{S}_{t}\bm{F}_{t}}_{\text{forget}}\;-\;\underbrace{\beta_{t}\big(\bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}_{t}\bm{F}_{t}\,\bm{k}_{t}\big)\,\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}}_{\text{erase}}\;+\;\underbrace{c_{t}\,\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}}_{\text{write}},
(10)
so that forgetting, erasing, and writing are controlled by three separate
gates, 𝑭 t \bm{F}_{t} , β t \beta_{t} and c t c_{t} .
The standard coupled and the unit-target variant c t ≡ 1 c_{t}\equiv 1 are compared
in Appendix A6 , Table A3 .
Gates.
The erase gate β t \beta_{t} and the write gate c t c_{t} are per-head scalars obtained
from linear projections of the input followed by a sigmoid,
β t = σ ⁡ ( 𝒘 β ⊤ ​ 𝒙 t + b β ) ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) , c t = σ ⁡ ( 𝒘 c ⊤ ​ 𝒙 t + b c ) ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) , \beta_{t}=\sigma\big(\bm{w}_{\beta}^{\top}\bm{x}_{t}+b_{\beta}\big)\in(0,1),\qquad c_{t}=\sigma\big(\bm{w}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\top}\bm{x}_{t}+b_{\mathrm{c}}\big)\in(0,1),
(11)
both bounded by construction. The diagonal vector 𝐟 t \mathbf{f}_{t} of the forget gate
𝑭 t = diag ⁡ ( 𝐟 t ) \bm{F}_{t}=\operatorname{diag}(\mathbf{f}_{t}) is obtained by raising a sigmoid gate to a learned
positive per-head power, following the parameterization of Mamba
( Gu and Dao, 2024 ) and Gated DeltaNet-2 ( Hatamizadeh et al., 2026 )
𝐟 t = 𝝈 ​ ( − ( 𝒔 f ) t ) a = ( 1 − 𝝈 ⁡ ( ( 𝒔 f ) t ) ) a , ( 𝒔 f ) t = 𝑾 f ​ 𝒙 t + 𝒃 f ∈ ℝ d k , α = exp ⁡ ( a log ) ∈ ℝ > 0 , \mathbf{f}_{t}\;=\;\bm{\sigma}(-(\bm{s}_{\mathrm{f}})_{t})^{a}\;=\;\big(1-\bm{\sigma}((\bm{s}_{\mathrm{f}})_{t})\big)^{a},\ \ (\bm{s}_{\mathrm{f}})_{t}=\bm{W}_{\mathrm{f}}\bm{x}_{t}+\bm{b}_{\mathrm{f}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}},\ \ \alpha=\exp(a_{\log})\in\mathbb{R}_{>0},
(12)
where a log a_{\log} is a single learned scalar per head, broadcast over the d k d_{k}
key dimensions, and the exponent is taken component-wise. The sigmoid sets retention separately for each key direction as a function of
the input. At the same time, the exponent rescales the head’s retention horizon by 1 / a 1/a ,
so that heads can operate at widely different timescales without high
pre-activations. Since a log a_{\log} is learned in log space, the baseline
retention timescale spans orders of magnitude across heads, while within a
head the per-direction variation comes entirely from the input. The read-out
is gated by a sigmoid output gate
𝐨 t = 𝝈 ⁡ ( 𝑾 o ​ 𝒙 t + 𝒃 o ) ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) d v \mathbf{o}_{t}=\bm{\sigma}(\bm{W}_{\mathrm{o}}\bm{x}_{t}+\bm{b}_{\mathrm{o}})\in(0,1)^{d_{v}} .
Figure 3: Architecture of the KronLSTM block.
KronLSTM block.
Queries, keys, and values are linear projections of 𝒙 t \bm{x}_{t} , each followed by a
short causal depthwise convolution and a SiLU activation, and are then
ℓ 2 \ell_{2} -normalized with 𝒒 t , 𝒌 t ∈ ℝ d k \bm{q}_{t},\bm{k}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}} and 𝒗 t ∈ ℝ d v \bm{v}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}} . The memory
𝑺 t ∈ ℝ d v × d k \bm{S}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}\times d_{k}} is updated by ( 10 ) and read
with the query, 𝒉 ~ t = 𝑺 t ​ 𝒒 t ∈ ℝ d v \tilde{\bm{h}}_{t}=\bm{S}_{t}\bm{q}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}} . The read-out passes through
the sigmoid output gate and a per-head RMSNorm, and the concatenated heads are mixed by
the output projection,
𝒉 t = 𝑾 out ​ RMSNorm ​ ( 𝐨 t ⊙ 𝒉 ~ t ) . \bm{h}_{t}\;=\;\bm{W}_{\text{out}}\,\mathrm{RMSNorm}\big(\mathbf{o}_{t}\odot\tilde{\bm{h}}_{t}\big).
With ℓ 2 \ell_{2} -normalized keys and values and a sigmoid β t \beta_{t} , we have
β t ​ ‖ 𝒌 t ‖ 2 ​ ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ 2 = β t < 1 \beta_{t}\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}=\beta_{t}<1 , so every update is non-expansive and
the memory operates in the regime of Theorem 2 ; the forget
gate, with entries below one, only contracts it further.
The architecture of the KronLSTM block is shown in Figure 3 .
It carries no state
besides the memories 𝑺 t \bm{S}_{t} of its heads: there is no normalizer state and no
stabilizer state as in the mLSTM.
KronLSTM can serve as a drop-in replacement for the attention layer in a Transformer block,
analogous to mLSTM and sLSTM in the xLSTM architecture.
3 Experimental Validation
We experimentally validate the KronLSTM architecture on different benchmarks
with a focus on language modeling tasks.
We compare KronLSTM with the following recurrent linear attention architectures:
Gated DeltaNet (GDN) ( Yang et al., 2025 ) , Kimi Delta Attention (KDA) ( Kimi Team et al., 2025 ) , Mamba-3 ( Lahoti et al., 2026 ) , and Gated DeltaNet-2 (GDN-2) ( Hatamizadeh et al., 2026 ) .
Recent deployments of linear attention in frontier models follow two hybrid-setting paradigms: either interleave the linear layers with a small number of softmax attention layers
( NVIDIA, 2025 ; Merrill et al., 2026 ; Kimi Team et al., 2025 ) or use sliding-window attention (SWA) instead ( Cabannes et al., 2026 ; Hatamizadeh et al., 2026 ) . The advantage of the latter is keeping the memory footprint constant with respect to the context length.
Therefore, we evaluate every architecture in two settings: as a pure linear attention model, and as a hybrid with SWA layers. For the hybrid setting, we employ a window size of 2048 at a 5 : 1 5{:}1 ratio
of linear attention to SWA layers.
All models are pretrained on the FineWeb-Edu dataset using the Llama2 tokenizer ( Touvron et al., 2023b ) .
We train at a context of 8k, motivated by previous works ( Guo et al., 2026 ; Beck et al., 2025a ; Beck et al., 2026 ) , where the throughput advantage of linear attention compared to the softmax attention emerges from 8k context onward. We evaluate the models within and extrapolating outside their context length (Section 3.2 ). The experiments in the following sections show 1.5B parameter models trained on 100B tokens. In Appendix A6 , we provide a set of experiments at 400M model size.
3.1 Language Modeling
We assess the performance of KronLSTM compared to leading linear attention variants on standard language modeling benchmarks, concretely on five commonsense reasoning tasks (HellaSwag ( Zellers et al., 2019 ) , PIQA ( Bisk et al., 2020 ) , ARC-easy and
ARC-challenge ( Clark et al., 2018 ) , WinoGrande ( Sakaguchi et al., 2021 ) ) and on LAMBADA ( Paperno et al., 2016 ) , reporting accuracy on all six tasks, their average (Avg-6), and LAMBADA perplexity. Table 1 shows the results of both linear and hybrid models.
Among all competitors, KronLSTM has the highest average accuracy over the six tasks
and the lowest perplexity on LAMBADA.
As a linear model, KronLSTM performs best on five out of six tasks
and improves on average on the strongest competitor, KDA, by 1.6 accuracy points.
The hybrid setting confirms KronLSTM’s performance advantage, leading on four of six tasks and achieving the lowest perplexity on LAMBADA.
Hybrid models with sliding-window attention change the averages by at most about one accuracy point. While mLSTM, Mamba-3 and GDN-2 benefit most from adding SWA, GDN and KDA change only minimally; KronLSTM remains robust, leading among all configurations.

Avg-6 is the mean accuracy over the five commonsense reasoning tasks and LAMBADA.

3.2 Retrieval Capabilities
On long-context tasks, the Kronecker Delta Rule should improve the most over previous update rules, as interference grows with context length.
We assess the performance of KronLSTM on two benchmarks with different context lengths.
The first benchmark is the real-world recall suite of Arora et al. (2024) (SWDE, SQuAD, FDA,
TriviaQA, NQ, DROP) for 2k context.
Results are given in Table 2 .
KronLSTM has the best average accuracy, and it
is the best method in three out of six tasks
in both pure linear attention models and hybrid models.
In general, hybrid models using SWA gain five to nine accuracy points.
The second benchmark is the RULER
needle-in-a-haystack (NIAH) tasks ( Hsieh et al., 2024 ) where
models are evaluated at 4k, 8k and 16k context length,
with 16k assessing the extrapolation capabilities of the models.
Results are presented in Table 3 .
Among all linear attention models,
KronLSTM is the best or second-best method across all sequence lengths,
except for task S1.
KronLSTM has the highest accuracy on S3 and MQ tasks
across all sequence lengths.
Among all hybrid models, KronLSTM achieves the highest accuracy
(averaged over all lengths) on all tasks
and has the highest accuracy at 16k on each task, confirming its strong long context capabilities.
The improvement of KronLSTM over competitors like Gated DeltaNet
is consistent with
the interference analysis of Property A9 .
On S2 and S3, whose needles are unique sentences embedded in
natural language text, KronLSTM outperforms GDN except for S2 at 16k.
For hybrid models, KronLSTM outranks GDN-2 and Mamba-3 by 15.2 accuracy points on S2 at 4k. KronLSTM’s margin increases on MQ and MV with sequence length.
On MQ, KronLSTM’s lead distance grows from 2.4 to 4.1 accuracy points when
increasing the context from 4k to 16k.
On MV, KronLSTM is 3.5 accuracy points behind the best method, GDN,
for 4k context, while KronLSTM is the leading method for
8k and 16k, with a 1.1 and 3.8 accuracy point lead, respectively.

For longer contexts, the memory updates of GDN more frequently interfere with
already stored content and perturb it, resulting in more degraded performance at 16k.
In contrast, the Kronecker Delta Rule is less invasive and performs better
at longer contexts.
The exception is S1, where KronLSTM underperforms.
S1 is the most artificial task with frequently repeated noise sentences,
which is unrealistic for real-world text.
We attribute the performance drop of the KronLSTM in this task
to the residual accumulation for recurring keys.
Hybrid models with SWA have a considerably smaller S1 accuracy, except KronLSTM.
The hybrid models containing the baseline linear attention layers reach at most 51.8, 26.6, and 12.6 accuracy for 4k, 8k, and 16k
context length, respectively.
These accuracies closely match the fraction of the context covered by the attention window, indicating that the needle is primarily retrieved when it lies within the window, with Mamba-3 and mLSTM dropping to zero at 16k.
The results suggest limited reliance on the recurrent architecture’s memory. KronLSTM exceeds these accuracies on average across all context lengths and achieves the best performance across all six tasks at 16k.
Section 3.3 examines how these methods perform
after context extension.
3.3 Long Context Extension
In applications, language models do not extrapolate to longer context but
are trained on them, typically by continued pretraining at increased context length
( Fu et al., 2024 ; Gao et al., 2024 ) .
This stage unlocks retrieval far beyond the original pretraining length ( Fu et al., 2024 ) .
Linear attention models have no positional encoding to adapt; rather, they must learn to keep more associations over longer time spans.
We therefore branch the 1.5B linear KronLSTM and GDN-2 runs at 75B tokens and train them for the
remaining 25B tokens at a sequence length of 32k on a long-document mixture
derived from ProLong-64K ( Gao et al., 2024 ) , so that the context-length-extended
models are token-matched to the 100B models of the previous sections.
GDN-2 serves as a reference model in this experiment since it also decouples erase and write.
Further details on training are described in Appendix A7.1 . Table 4 shows the commonsense reasoning, LAMBADA, and recall results of the extended models.
Both models benefit from length extension on the reasoning and recall tasks,
and perplexity improves by about 4 perplexity points.
KronLSTM keeps its lead: it has the higher average accuracy and the lower perplexity,
and it is better on five of the six recall tasks.

Results for context extension at the RULER tasks are given in Figure 4 and Table A4 .
As expected, KronLSTM shows its advantage by extending the training context length. While both architectures perform strongly on single-needle retrieval at shorter contexts, KronLSTM increasingly outperforms GDN-2 on the more challenging NIAH tasks, ranging from 8k to 32k. At 16k, this advantage is particularly pronounced on S-3 and MV, with KronLSTM leading on most tasks except S1, where GDN-2 remains stronger. At 32k, KronLSTM maintains its lead across all five tasks, although both architectures experience a substantial drop in retrieval accuracy compared to 16k.
The gap between the two models widens with haystack size, as predicted by the interference analysis in Section 2.1 : longer contexts accumulate more interference, and KronLSTM’s update rule suffers less of it.
Figure 4: GDN-2 and KronLSTM results on NIAH for 32k context extension for linear and SWA-hybrid models.
3.4 State Tracking Capabilities
KronLSTM can perform state tracking by setting β t = 2 ​ σ ​ ( 𝒘 β ⊤ ​ 𝒙 t + b β ) ∈ ( 0 , 2 ) \beta_{t}=2\sigma(\bm{w}_{\beta}^{\top}\bm{x}_{t}+b_{\beta})\in(0,2) , as described in Grazzi et al. (2025) for DeltaNet.
We evaluate KronLSTM’s state tracking capabilities on
parity, S 3 S_{3} permutation, and modulo arithmetic.
We train up to sequence length 128 and evaluate up to 1024.
Figure 5 shows that KronLSTM has similar state tracking capabilities as Gated DeltaNet on the parity and S 3 S_{3} tasks, both ahead of Mamba-3, which wins on modulo arithmetic.
Figure 5: State tracking results for Gated DeltaNet (GDN), Mamba-3, and KronLSTM.
3.5 Training Efficiency
Figure 3.5 reports end-to-end training throughput of the purely linear 1.5B models on a single NVIDIA H100 (80 GB), following Hatamizadeh et al. (2026) : each step processes 16,384 tokens, split from 2K × \times 8 to 16K × \times 1 (sequence length × \times batch size), and all models share the same training stack. KronLSTM reaches 36.8–44.5K tokens/s, 11–18% more than GDN-2 at every shape and within 4–8% of KDA up to 8K × \times 2. GDN, whose scalar decay admits the cheapest kernels, remains the fastest. Throughput is nearly flat while a step holds at least two sequences; at 16K × \times 1 all models slow down, since the inter-chunk recurrence parallelizes only over sequences and heads.
Figure 6: Training throughput of KronLSTM against baselines on a single H100-80GB.
4 Conclusion
We have introduced the Kronecker Delta Rule, an update rule for associative matrix memories that corrects a scalar association along a single direction, and KronLSTM, which embeds this rule in a gated linear attention mixer.
We proved that the memory dynamics converge to a unique steady state and that the expected memory norm is bounded at every step.
KronLSTM has the best average accuracy on commonsense reasoning and real-world recall, both as a linear model and as a hybrid.
Averaged over the context lengths, it is the best or second-best linear model on every natural-text RULER task,
and on S1 it is the only one that retrieves needles beyond the sliding attention window in hybrid models. After extending the context to 32k, KronLSTM outperforms GDN-2 on all natural-text retrieval tasks from 16k onward.
Future work includes scaling up KronLSTM and training hybrid models containing full softmax attention layers.
AI use statement
The Kronecker Delta Rule and the KronLSTM architecture originated with the authors without AI assistance. LLMs were used to assist in developing the theoretical results and in implementing the method. Claude Fable 5 and Claude Fable 5.1 assisted in deriving the properties of the Kronecker Delta Rule and the convergence and memory norm bounds of Section 2.1 and Appendix A3 ; every statement and proof was checked independently by the authors, who take full responsibility for their correctness. Claude Fable 5 was further used to examine candidate formulations of the Kronecker Delta Rule, in particular the coupled, decoupled, and unit-target write variants, for their viability; the choice of the form in ( 10 ) based on was made by the authors on the basis of ablation studies such as in Appendix A6 . Fable 5.1 was used to assist in deriving the chunkwise-parallel formulation of KronLSTM. The formulation was subsequently checked thoroughly by our team. Claude Fable 5 and Claude Fable 5.1 were used to optimize the chunkwise-parallel and recurrent kernels of KronLSTM; the resulting kernels were numerically verified against naive reference implementations written by the authors. We did not use generative AI tools to interpret results or to design experiments. We have not used generative AI tools to generate synthetic data or to propose hypotheses, and translation, dataset cleaning and reformatting, and qualitative or thematic data analysis do not apply to this work. Additionally, we used Claude Fable 5 and Claude Opus 5 to draft parts of the manuscript and to edit it for readability. All AI-drafted text was revised by the authors. We have reviewed all AI-assisted work and take responsibility for the final content of this work, including text, claims, and artifacts produced with the aid of generative AI.

Appendix A2 Related Work
To the best of our knowledge, no existing work uses exactly this update that is a scalar
bilinear read-out 𝒗 ⊤ ​ 𝑺 ​ 𝒌 \bm{v}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{k} regressed onto the constant target 1 1 . The established
delta-rule linear attention family uses the vector read-out 𝑺 ​ 𝒌 → 𝒗 \bm{S}\bm{k}\to\bm{v} . The rule studied
here is therefore the bilinear-readout, scalar-target member of the
delta-rule. The constraints are
⟨ 𝒗 ​ 𝒌 ⊤ , 𝑺 ⟩ F = 1 {{\left\langle\bm{v}\bm{k}^{\top},\bm{S}\right\rangle}}_{\mathrm{F}}=1 rather than 𝑺 ​ 𝒌 = 𝒗 \bm{S}\bm{k}=\bm{v} .
A2.1 Long Short-Term Memory
KronLSTM belongs to the xLSTM ( Beck et al., 2024 )
family that revisits LSTM ( Hochreiter, 1991 ; Hochreiter and Schmidhuber, 1997 )
for the era of large language models (LLMs) with two modifications:
exponential gating and a redesigned memory.
mLSTM replaces the scalar cell state by a matrix memory
and drops memory mixing, making the recurrence fully
parallelizable over the sequence.
mLSTM enables recurrent LLMs with fast, constant-memory inference
at scale ( Beck et al., 2025a ) , and
Beck et al. (2026) establish scaling behavior competitive with
Transformers at linear time complexity.
xLSTM has also been applied
to language modeling ( Beck et al., 2025b ) ,
vision ( Alkin et al., 2025 ) , biological and chemical sequences
( Schmidinger et al., 2025 ) , and robotics ( Schmied et al., 2025 ) .
The matrix memory of mLSTM can be viewed as a classical
correlation-based associative memory ( Kohonen, 1972 ; Anderson, 1972 )
embedded within LSTM-style gating.
In addition to providing an efficient mechanism for storing and
retrieving associations, the exponential gates allow mLSTM to
revise storage decisions. This distinguishes mLSTM from purely
accumulative linear attention mechanisms.
KronLSTM follows this line of research by retaining the matrix memory
and the ability to revise storage decisions, while introducing a
different memory-update rule.
A central challenge of recurrent architectures with growing memory is
maintaining stable memory dynamics over long sequences.
The original LSTM uses a bounded squashing function for the exposed
cell state, while xLSTM introduces a normalizer state and a stabilizer
state to control the scale of its matrix memory and retrievals.
However, normalization based on ratios of large quantities can lead
to numerical and optimization difficulties, particularly in large
networks ( Beck et al., 2025b ) . MC-LSTM ( Hoedt et al., 2021 ) instead redistributes its cell state with
column-stochastic matrices, which conserve mass and never amplify the state.
These considerations motivate alternative matrix-memory update rules
that retain memory revision while providing stable memory dynamics.
The Kronecker Delta Rule addresses this issue and
provides convergence to a steady-state distribution with a
norm-bounded memory in expectation. Furthermore, KronLSTM yields advantageous
gradient flow over time compared with mLSTM
( Hochreiter et al., 2000 ) .
A2.2 The delta-rule linear attention family
The direct ancestor is the fast weight programmer perspective of
Schlag et al. (2021) , who introduced the delta-rule write
𝑺 t + 1 = 𝑺 t − β ⁡ ( 𝑺 t ​ 𝒌 t − 𝒗 t ) ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ \bm{S}_{t+1}=\bm{S}_{t}-\beta\,(\bm{S}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}-\bm{v}_{t})\,\bm{k}_{t}^{\top} into linear attention
( Katharopoulos et al., 2020 ) , building on the fast-weight programmers of the
early 1990s ( Schmidhuber, 1992 ) . Yang et al. (2024) (DeltaNet)
made this rule hardware-efficient by exploiting the WY representation of the resulting
products of generalized Householder matrices. This is exactly the product structure the bilinear
rule inherits,
with the Kronecker product 𝒌 t ⊗ 𝒗 t \bm{k}_{t}\otimes\bm{v}_{t} taking the role of 𝒌 t \bm{k}_{t} . Subsequent extensions
include Gated DeltaNet ( Yang et al., 2025 ) , which adds a scalar forget gate
α t ​ 𝑺 t \alpha_{t}\bm{S}_{t} ; RWKV-7 ( Peng et al., 2025 ) , which employs a generalized delta rule with
a decoupled in-context learning rate; and DeltaProduct ( Siems et al., 2025 ) ,
which applies multiple Householder factors per token to improve state tracking. This is directly
relevant to the negative-eigenvalue regime β ​ ‖ 𝒂 t ‖ 2 ∈ ( 1 , 2 ) \beta\left\lVert\bm{a}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}\in(1,2) of the
transition factors 𝑰 − β ​ 𝒂 t ​ 𝒂 t ⊤ \bm{I}-\beta\bm{a}_{t}\bm{a}_{t}^{\top} , which allows state-tracking.
A2.3 The online-learning perspective
Longhorn ( Liu et al., 2025 ) derives state-space recurrences explicitly as
closed-form solutions of per-token online learning objectives; the bilinear rule fits
this template with the objective 1 2 ​ ( 𝒗 ⊤ ​ 𝑺 ​ 𝒌 − 1 ) 2 \tfrac{1}{2}\,(\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{k}-1)^{2} . Test-time training (TTT)
layers ( Sun et al., 2024 ) and Titans ( Behrouz et al., 2025 ) treat the memory as a
model trained by gradient descent at inference time. Most relevant as a unifying
framework is the Miras taxonomy of Behrouz et al. (2026) , which classifies memory
architectures by their attentional-bias objective, retention mechanism, and optimizer.
The new bilinear rule occupies a new place of that taxonomy: it is a bilinear attentional
bias with a constant scalar target. A recent related observation is that test-time training
with key–value binding is secretly linear attention ( Liu et al., 2026 ) . The
mesa-optimization line ( von Oswald et al., 2023 ) provides the complementary
least-squares-solver perspective on such layers.
A2.4 Classical update and learning rules
When vectorized, the rule is the Widrow–Hoff LMS / delta rule ( Widrow and Hoff, 1960 )
applied to the Kronecker product input 𝒂 t = 𝒌 t ⊗ 𝒗 t \bm{a}_{t}=\bm{k}_{t}\otimes\bm{v}_{t} with target 1 1 .
The stability range
β ​ ‖ 𝒂 t ‖ 2 ∈ ( 0 , 2 ) \beta\left\lVert\bm{a}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}\in(0,2) is the classical LMS step-size condition. The
projection interpretation is Kaczmarz’s method ( Kaczmarz, 1937 ) : with the Newton step
β ⋆ = 1 / ( ‖ 𝒌 ‖ 2 ​ ‖ 𝒗 ‖ 2 ) \beta^{\star}=1/(\left\lVert\bm{k}\right\rVert^{2}\,\left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert^{2}) , the update is the orthogonal
projection onto the hyperplane { 𝑺 : ⟨ 𝒗 ​ 𝒌 ⊤ , 𝑺 ⟩ F = 1 } \{\bm{S}:{{\left\langle\bm{v}\bm{k}^{\top},\bm{S}\right\rangle}}_{\mathrm{F}}=1\} , and general
β \beta gives relaxed projections. The modern convergence theory, including the linear
rate under random sampling of constraints, is due to Strohmer and Vershynin (2009) . The
connections between Kaczmarz iterations, stochastic gradient descent, and learning are
surveyed by Chen (2018) . Fejér monotonicity and relaxed projection
methods are standard in the projection-methods literature ( Cegielski, 2012 ) .
A2.5 Relation to cognitive and neuroscience
Because 𝑺 \bm{S} scores key–value pairs rather than retrieving values, the closest relative
in the cognitive and neuroscience literature is familiarity (recognition) memory: the
anti-Hebbian model of familiarity discrimination in perirhinal cortex of
Bogacz and Brown (2003) shows that scoring memories attain a far higher capacity than
retrieval memories, which is the same d 2 d^{2} -versus- d d capacity separation derived for the
bilinear rule. The classical retrieval-side background is given by correlation matrix
memories ( Kohonen, 1972 ; Anderson, 1972 ) for the Hebbian write, and by
delta-rule / pseudoinverse associative memories for capacity under Widrow–Hoff
learning ( Prados and Kak, 1989 ) .
Appendix A3 Kronecker Delta Rule: Setting, Name, Properties
A3.1 Setup and notation.
We use the notation of the main paper (Section 2.1 ), specialized
to d k = d v = : d d_{k}=d_{v}=:d as in our experiments (where d q ​ k = d v d_{qk}=d_{v} ); the general
d v × d k d_{v}\times d_{k} case is identical with d 2 d^{2} replaced by d k ​ d v d_{k}d_{v} .
Let 𝑺 t ∈ ℝ d × d \bm{S}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d} be the memory matrix, 𝒌 t ∈ ℝ d \bm{k}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d} the key and
𝒗 t ∈ ℝ d \bm{v}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d} the value at step t t , c t ∈ ℝ c_{t}\in\mathbb{R} a scalar target, and β t \beta_{t}
a step size. The scalar 𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑺 t ​ 𝒌 t \bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}_{t}\bm{k}_{t} measures how strongly the memory
associates 𝒌 t \bm{k}_{t} with 𝒗 t \bm{v}_{t} ; the Kronecker Delta Rule corrects it towards the
target c t c_{t} along the outer product 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ \bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top} ,
𝑺 t + 1 = 𝑺 t − β t ​ ( 𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑺 t ​ 𝒌 t − c t ) ​ 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ , \boxed{\;\bm{S}_{t+1}\;=\;\bm{S}_{t}\;-\;\beta_{t}\big(\bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}\,-\,c_{t}\big)\,\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}\,,\;}
(A1)
which is Eq. ( 3 ) of the main paper. For the analysis, we abbreviate the
scalar read-out and its residual,
y t \displaystyle y_{t}\;
: = 𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑺 t ​ 𝒌 t \displaystyle:=\;\bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}
(scalar read-out) , \displaystyle\text{(scalar read-out)},
(A2)
ψ t \displaystyle\psi_{t}\;
: = y t − c t \displaystyle:=\;y_{t}-c_{t}
(residual) , \displaystyle\text{(residual)},
(A3)
so that the update reads compactly 𝑺 t + 1 = 𝑺 t − β t ​ r t ​ 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ \bm{S}_{t+1}=\bm{S}_{t}-\beta_{t}\,r_{t}\,\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top} .
The target sequence c t c_{t} subsumes different variants:
c t ≡ 1 c_{t}\equiv 1 is the basic Kronecker Delta Rule with target 1 1 ,
c t ≡ c > 0 c_{t}\equiv c>0 the Constant-Target Kronecker Delta Rule, c t = ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ 2 c_{t}=\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert^{2} the
Norm-Target Kronecker Delta Rule, and c t = ‖ 𝒌 t ‖ ​ ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ c_{t}=\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert the
Double-Norm-Target Kronecker Delta Rule. The target c t c_{t} may also be produced by a
gate or a network, so that what is written becomes itself input-dependent.
Throughout we use 𝒗 ⊤ ​ 𝑺 ​ 𝒌 = Tr ⁡ ( 𝑺 ​ 𝒌 ​ 𝒗 ⊤ ) = ⟨ 𝒗 ​ 𝒌 ⊤ , 𝑺 ⟩ F \bm{v}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{k}=\mathbf{\mathrm{Tr}}\!\big(\bm{S}\,\bm{k}\bm{v}^{\top}\big)={{\left\langle\bm{v}\bm{k}^{\top},\bm{S}\right\rangle}}_{\mathrm{F}} .
A3.2 Name Kronecker Delta Rule
We use the Kronecker product
𝒂 t = 𝒌 t ⊗ 𝒗 t \bm{a}_{t}\;=\;\bm{k}_{t}\otimes\bm{v}_{t}
(A4)
in a vectorized update rule.
𝒂 t \bm{a}_{t} is a vector of length d 2 d^{2} , where
the vector 𝒗 t \bm{v}_{t} is multiplied by each component of
𝒌 t \bm{k}_{t} and then stacked to one vector.
However, we have 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ = 𝒗 t ⊗ 𝒌 t ⊤ \bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}=\bm{v}_{t}\otimes\bm{k}_{t}^{\top} .
Let 𝒔 = vec ⁡ ( 𝑺 ) ∈ ℝ d 2 \bm{s}=\vecop(\bm{S})\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{2}} and 𝒂 t = 𝒌 t ⊗ 𝒗 t ∈ ℝ d 2 \bm{a}_{t}=\bm{k}_{t}\otimes\bm{v}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{2}} ,
so that 𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑺 ​ 𝒌 t = 𝒂 t ⊤ ​ 𝒔 \bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{k}_{t}=\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\bm{s} . The rule becomes
𝒔 t + 1 = ( 𝑰 − β ​ 𝒂 t ​ 𝒂 t ⊤ ) ​ 𝒔 t + β ​ c t ​ 𝒂 t , \bm{s}_{t+1}\;=\;\big(\bm{I}-\beta\,\bm{a}_{t}\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\big)\,\bm{s}_{t}+\beta\,c_{t}\,\bm{a}_{t},
(A5)
which is exactly Delta Rule with Kronecker product input 𝒂 t \bm{a}_{t}
and target c t c_{t} .
A3.3 Properties
Property A1 (Memory Space is Hilbert Space and Kronecker Update Rule is a Projection onto a Hyperplane) .
The memory space ℝ d × d \mathbb{R}^{d\times d} is a Hilbert space
endowed with the Frobenius inner product
⟨ 𝑿 , 𝒀 ⟩ F = Tr ⁡ ( 𝑿 ⊤ ​ 𝒀 ) , {{\left\langle\bm{X},\bm{Y}\right\rangle}}_{\mathrm{F}}\,=\,\mathbf{\mathrm{Tr}}\left(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{Y}\right)\,,
where 𝐗 , 𝐘 ∈ ℝ d × d \bm{X},\bm{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d} .
Hence the target condition
𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑺 ​ 𝒌 t = c t \bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{k}_{t}\,=\,c_{t}
is a hyperplane in this Hilbert space.
The update
𝑺 t + 1 = 𝑺 t − β ⁡ ( 𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑺 t ​ 𝒌 t − c t ) ​ 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ \bm{S}_{t+1}\,=\,\bm{S}_{t}-\beta\left(\bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}-c_{t}\right)\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}
is a orthogonal projection onto it, if
β = 1 ‖ 𝒌 t ‖ 2 2 ​ ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ 2 2 . \beta\,=\,\frac{1}{\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}\,.
The update
𝑺 t + 1 = 𝑺 t − β ⁡ ( 𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑺 t ​ 𝒌 t − c t ) ​ 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ \bm{S}_{t+1}\,=\,\bm{S}_{t}-\beta\left(\bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}-c_{t}\right)\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}
is a relaxed orthogonal projection onto it, with relaxation
parameter
ω t = β ​ ‖ 𝒌 t ‖ 2 2 ​ ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ 2 2 . \omega_{t}\,=\,\beta\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\,.
Property A2 (Exact online gradient descent: a bilinear delta rule
with target c t c_{t} ) .
The rule ( A1 ) is stochastic gradient descent
with step size β \beta on the instantaneous loss
L t ​ ( 𝑺 ) = 1 2 ​ ( 𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑺 ​ 𝒌 t − c t ) 2 , ∇ S L t ​ ( 𝑺 ) = ( 𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑺 ​ 𝒌 t − c t ) ​ 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ . \mathrm{L}_{t}(\bm{S})=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(\bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{k}_{t}-c_{t}\big)^{2},\qquad\nabla_{S}\,\mathrm{L}_{t}(\bm{S})=\big(\bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{k}_{t}-c_{t}\big)\,\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}.
(A6)
It differs from DeltaNet, which performs SGD on 1 2 ​ ‖ 𝐒 ​ 𝐤 t − 𝐯 t ‖ 2 \tfrac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{S}\bm{k}_{t}-\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}
with update 𝐒 t + 1 = 𝐒 t − β ⁡ ( 𝐒 t ​ 𝐤 t − 𝐯 t ) ​ 𝐤 t ⊤ \bm{S}_{t+1}=\bm{S}_{t}-\beta(\bm{S}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}-\bm{v}_{t})\bm{k}_{t}^{\top} :
there the target is the vector 𝐯 t \bm{v}_{t} (imposing d d constraints
per pair),
whereas here the target is the scalar c t c_{t} and
the pair ( 𝐤 t , 𝐯 t ) (\bm{k}_{t},\bm{v}_{t}) jointly forms the input,
imposing a single bilinear constraint 𝐯 t ⊤ ​ 𝐒 ​ 𝐤 t = c t \bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{k}_{t}=c_{t} per pair.
Property A3 (Superposition: the memory is linear in the target sequence) .
For a fixed pair sequence ( 𝐤 t , 𝐯 t ) t = 1 T (\bm{k}_{t},\bm{v}_{t})_{t=1}^{T} and step size
β \beta , the map from the target sequence
𝐜 = ( c 1 , … , c T ) \bm{c}=(c_{1},\dots,c_{T}) to the final memory 𝐒 T ​ ( 𝐜 ) \bm{S}_{T}(\bm{c}) with
𝐒 0 = 𝟎 \bm{S}_{0}=\bm{0} is linear:
𝑺 T ​ ( 𝒄 + 𝒄 ′ ) = 𝑺 T ​ ( 𝒄 ) + 𝑺 T ​ ( 𝒄 ′ ) , 𝑺 T ​ ( α ​ 𝒄 ) = α ​ 𝑺 T ​ ( 𝒄 ) , \bm{S}_{T}(\bm{c}+\bm{c}^{\prime})\;=\;\bm{S}_{T}(\bm{c})\,+\,\bm{S}_{T}(\bm{c}^{\prime}),\qquad\bm{S}_{T}(\alpha\,\bm{c})\;=\;\alpha\ \bm{S}_{T}(\bm{c})\,,
(A7)
explicitly, with 𝐚 t = 𝐤 t ⊗ 𝐯 t \bm{a}_{t}=\bm{k}_{t}\otimes\bm{v}_{t} ,
vec ⁡ ( 𝑺 T ​ ( 𝒄 ) ) = β ​ ∑ t = 1 T c t ​ [ ∏ τ = t + 1 T ( 𝑰 − β ​ 𝒂 τ ​ 𝒂 τ ⊤ ) ] ​ 𝒂 t . \vecop\big(\bm{S}_{T}(\bm{c})\big)\;=\;\beta\sum_{t=1}^{T}c_{t}\Bigg[\prod_{\tau=t+1}^{T}\big(\bm{I}-\beta\bm{a}_{\tau}\bm{a}_{\tau}^{\top}\big)\Bigg]\bm{a}_{t}\,.
(A8)
Memories written with different target sequences over the same pairs
superpose exactly. The pure-gain homogeneity of the constant-target
rule ( 𝐒 t ( c ) = c ​ 𝐒 t ( 1 ) \bm{S}_{t}^{(c)}=c\,\bm{S}_{t}^{(1)} ) is the special case 𝐜 = c ​ 1 \bm{c}=c\,\bm{1} . The pair sequence determines where the memory can
write (the reachable subspace span ⁡ { 𝐚 1 , … , 𝐚 T } \spanop\{\bm{a}_{1},\dots,\bm{a}_{T}\} and the
interference pattern); the target sequence linearly selects what
is written into it.
Property A4 (Per-step reparameterization to the unit target) .
For every step with c t ≠ 0 c_{t}\neq 0 , rule ( A1 ) with pair
( 𝐤 t , 𝐯 t ) (\bm{k}_{t},\bm{v}_{t}) and step β \beta coincides exactly with the
unit-target rule applied to the rescaled value 𝐯 ~ t = 𝐯 t / c t \tilde{\bm{v}}_{t}=\bm{v}_{t}/c_{t} with the time-varying effective step β ~ t = β ​ c t 2 \tilde{\beta}_{t}=\beta\,c_{t}^{2} :
𝑺 − β ⁡ ( 𝒗 ⊤ ​ 𝑺 ​ 𝒌 − c t ) ​ 𝒗 ​ 𝒌 ⊤ = 𝑺 − β ~ t ​ ( 𝒗 ~ ⊤ ​ 𝑺 ​ 𝒌 − 1 ) ​ 𝒗 ~ ​ 𝒌 ⊤ . \bm{S}-\beta\big(\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{k}-c_{t}\big)\bm{v}\bm{k}^{\top}\;=\;\bm{S}-\tilde{\beta}_{t}\,\big(\tilde{\bm{v}}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{k}-1\big)\,\tilde{\bm{v}}\bm{k}^{\top}.
(A9)
In contrast to the constant-target case, the effective step size now
varies over time, so the time-varying rule is not globally a
reparameterized unit-target rule; steps with c t = 0 c_{t}=0 have no
unit-target counterpart at all (see
Property A5 (iii)).
Property A5 (Distinguished targets) .
Three choices of c t c_{t} are structurally special.
1.
(i) Norm target, c t = ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ 2 c_{t}=\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert^{2} . The residual becomes
the value-projected retrieval error r t = 𝒗 t ⊤ ​ ( 𝑺 t ​ 𝒌 t − 𝒗 t ) r_{t}=\bm{v}_{t}^{\top}(\bm{S}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}-\bm{v}_{t}) ; every exact retrieval memory ( 𝑺 ​ 𝒌 i = 𝒗 i \bm{S}\bm{k}_{i}=\bm{v}_{i} ) satisfies all constraints, and in the autoassociative case
𝒌 t = 𝒗 t \bm{k}_{t}=\bm{v}_{t} the identity 𝑺 = 𝑰 \bm{S}=\bm{I} is a universal fixed point.
(ii) Correlation target, c t = 𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝒌 t c_{t}=\bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{k}_{t} . The identity
𝑺 = 𝑰 \bm{S}=\bm{I} is a universal fixed point even heteroassociatively: 𝒗 ⊤ ​ 𝑰 ​ 𝒌 = 𝒗 ⊤ ​ 𝒌 \bm{v}^{\top}\bm{I}\bm{k}=\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{k} for all
pairs. The rule then learns deviations of the bilinear map from the
standard inner product, i.e. 𝑺 \bm{S} is naturally parameterized
around the identity, and the read-out 𝒗 ⊤ ​ 𝑺 ​ 𝒌 − 𝒗 ⊤ ​ 𝒌 \bm{v}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{k}-\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{k} measures the learned (in-context) part of the
similarity.
(iii) Erasure target, c t = 0 c_{t}=0 . The update drives 𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑺 ​ 𝒌 t → 0 \bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{k}_{t}\to 0 : with the Newton step β ⋆ = 1 / ( ‖ 𝒌 t ‖ 2 ​ ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ 2 ) \beta^{\star}=1/(\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}) the association is deleted in one shot, while
every read-out with 𝒌 ′ ⟂ 𝒌 t \bm{k}^{\prime}\perp\bm{k}_{t} or 𝒗 ′ ⟂ 𝒗 t \bm{v}^{\prime}\perp\bm{v}_{t} is
exactly unchanged. Hence, c t = 0 c_{t}=0 is a targeted unlearning of a single
key–value binding. Alternating write targets ( c t ≠ 0 c_{t}\neq 0 ) and
erasure targets ( c t = 0 c_{t}=0 ) turns the memory into an editable
scalar store (see also Property A10 on
overwriting).
Property A6 (One-step error contraction and stability range for β \beta ) .
The target never enters the homogeneous part of the dynamics.
Re-reading with the same pair and the same target after one update,
𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑺 t + 1 ​ 𝒌 t = y t − β ​ r t ​ ‖ 𝒌 t ‖ 2 ​ ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ 2 \displaystyle\qquad\;\bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}_{t+1}\bm{k}_{t}\;=\;y_{t}-\beta\,r_{t}\,\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}\,\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}
(A10)
⟺ 𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑺 t + 1 ​ 𝒌 t − c t = y t − c t − β ​ r t ​ ‖ 𝒌 t ‖ 2 ​ ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ 2 \displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}_{t+1}\bm{k}_{t}-c_{t}\;=\;y_{t}-c_{t}-\beta\,r_{t}\,\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}\,\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}
(A11)
⟺ r t + 1 = r t − β ​ r t ​ ‖ 𝒌 t ‖ 2 ​ ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ 2 \displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow r_{t+1}\;=\;r_{t}-\beta\,r_{t}\,\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}\,\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}
(A12)
⟺ r t + 1 = ( 1 − β ​ ‖ 𝒌 t ‖ 2 ​ ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ 2 ) ​ r t , \displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow r_{t+1}\;=\;\big(1-\beta\,\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}\big)\,r_{t}\;,
(A13)
where r t + 1 r_{t+1} is computed for the same ( 𝐤 t , 𝐯 t , c t ) (\bm{k}_{t},\bm{v}_{t},c_{t}) .
The residual contracts if and only if
0 < β < 2 ‖ 𝒌 t ‖ 2 ​ ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ 2 , 0\;<\;\beta\;<\;\frac{2}{\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}\,\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}},
(A14)
and the loss shrinks by
the factor ( 1 − β ​ ‖ 𝐤 t ‖ 2 ​ ‖ 𝐯 t ‖ 2 ) 2 \big(1-\beta\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}\,\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}\big)^{2} .
Since L t \mathrm{L}_{t} is quadratic along the ray 𝐒 − η ​ r t ​ 𝐯 t ​ 𝐤 t ⊤ \bm{S}-\eta\,r_{t}\,\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top} ,,
the choice
β ⋆ = 1 ‖ 𝒌 t ‖ 2 ​ ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ 2 \beta^{\star}\;=\;\frac{1}{\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}\,\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}}
(A15)
is a Newton step: the constraint 𝐯 t ⊤ ​ 𝐒 ​ 𝐤 t = c t \bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{k}_{t}=c_{t} is
interpolated exactly in one update, for every value of c t c_{t} .
Property A7 (Delta Rule / Widrow–Hoff LMS in vectorized form) .
Let 𝐬 = vec ⁡ ( 𝐒 ) ∈ ℝ d 2 \bm{s}=\vecop(\bm{S})\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{2}} and 𝐚 t = 𝐤 t ⊗ 𝐯 t ∈ ℝ d 2 \bm{a}_{t}=\bm{k}_{t}\otimes\bm{v}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{2}} the Kronecker product,
so that 𝐯 t ⊤ ​ 𝐒 ​ 𝐤 t = 𝐚 t ⊤ ​ 𝐬 \bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{k}_{t}=\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\bm{s} and ‖ 𝐚 t ‖ 2 = ‖ 𝐤 t ‖ 2 ​ ‖ 𝐯 t ‖ 2 \left\lVert\bm{a}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}=\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}\,\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert^{2} . The rule becomes
𝒔 t + 1 = ( 𝑰 − β ​ 𝒂 t ​ 𝒂 t ⊤ ) ​ 𝒔 t + β ​ c t ​ 𝒂 t , \bm{s}_{t+1}\;=\;\big(\bm{I}-\beta\,\bm{a}_{t}\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\big)\,\bm{s}_{t}\;+\;\beta\,c_{t}\,\bm{a}_{t}\,,
(A16)
which is exactly the Widrow–Hoff LMS/delta rule with input 𝐚 t \bm{a}_{t}
and target c t c_{t} . This is the classical setting of LMS, which regresses a
time-varying desired signal onto the input.
The transition matrix 𝐈 − β ​ 𝐚 t ​ 𝐚 t ⊤ \bm{I}-\beta\bm{a}_{t}\bm{a}_{t}^{\top} does not depend
on c t c_{t} : it has eigenvalue 1 − β ​ ‖ 𝐚 t ‖ 2 1-\beta\left\lVert\bm{a}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}
on span ⁡ ( 𝐚 t ) \spanop(\bm{a}_{t}) and eigenvalue 1 1 on the orthogonal complement
(dimension d 2 − 1 d^{2}-1 ).
Unrolling the recurrence,
𝒔 T = ∏ t = 1 T ( 𝑰 − β ​ 𝒂 t ​ 𝒂 t ⊤ ) ​ 𝒔 0 + β ​ ∑ t = 1 T c t ​ [ ∏ τ = t + 1 T ( 𝑰 − β ​ 𝒂 τ ​ 𝒂 τ ⊤ ) ] ​ 𝒂 t , \bm{s}_{T}\;=\;\prod_{t=1}^{T}\big(\bm{I}-\beta\bm{a}_{t}\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\big)\,\bm{s}_{0}\;+\;\beta\sum_{t=1}^{T}c_{t}\Bigg[\prod_{\tau=t+1}^{T}\big(\bm{I}-\beta\bm{a}_{\tau}\bm{a}_{\tau}^{\top}\big)\Bigg]\bm{a}_{t}\;,
(A17)
gives the same product-of-generalized-Householder structure as DeltaNet.
Hence, the WY-representation / chunkwise-parallel training techniques
apply verbatim with 𝐚 t = 𝐤 t ⊗ 𝐯 t \bm{a}_{t}=\bm{k}_{t}\otimes\bm{v}_{t} in place of 𝐤 t \bm{k}_{t} ,
and the targets c t c_{t} enter only the driving (inhomogeneous) term.
Expressivity consequence: for β ​ ‖ 𝐚 t ‖ 2 ∈ ( 1 , 2 ) \beta\left\lVert\bm{a}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}\in(1,2) the
factor has a negative eigenvalue (reaching − 1 -1 at
β ​ ‖ 𝐚 t ‖ 2 = 2 \beta\left\lVert\bm{a}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}=2 ),
the same mechanism that gives DeltaNet-style models state-tracking
power beyond linear RNNs restricted to nonnegative eigenvalues.
This is independent of the target sequence.
Property A8 (Homogeneous part of the update in non-expansive) .
For β ​ ‖ 𝐚 t ‖ 2 ∈ [ 0 , 2 ] \beta\left\lVert\bm{a}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}\in[0,2] every factor
𝐈 − β ​ 𝐚 t ​ 𝐚 t ⊤ \bm{I}-\beta\bm{a}_{t}\bm{a}_{t}^{\top} has spectral norm at most 1 1 , so the
homogeneous part of
the recurrence never amplifies and ‖ 𝐒 t ‖ \left\lVert\bm{S}_{t}\right\rVert cannot blow
up. In contrast the Hebbian write 𝐒 t + 1 = 𝐒 t + 𝐯 t ​ 𝐤 t ⊤ \bm{S}_{t+1}=\bm{S}_{t}+\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top} of
vanilla linear attention leads to states that grow
without bound. Moreover, the update is surgical: it modifies
𝐒 \bm{S} only along the rank-one direction 𝐯 t ​ 𝐤 t ⊤ \bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top} . For any
test pair with 𝐤 ′ ⟂ 𝐤 t \bm{k}^{\prime}\perp\bm{k}_{t} or 𝐯 ′ ⟂ 𝐯 t \bm{v}^{\prime}\perp\bm{v}_{t} the
read-out 𝐯 ′ ⁣ ⊤ ​ 𝐒 ​ 𝐤 ′ \bm{v}^{\prime\top}\bm{S}\bm{k}^{\prime} is exactly unchanged.
Property A9 (Doubly gated interference) .
For a previously stored pair ( 𝐤 i , 𝐯 i ) (\bm{k}_{i},\bm{v}_{i}) , an update with ( 𝐤 t , 𝐯 t , c t ) (\bm{k}_{t},\bm{v}_{t},c_{t}) perturbs the stored read-out by
Δ ⁡ ( 𝒗 i ⊤ ​ 𝑺 ​ 𝒌 i ) = − β ​ r t ​ ( 𝒗 i ⊤ ​ 𝒗 t ) ​ ( 𝒌 t ⊤ ​ 𝒌 i ) , \Delta\big(\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{k}_{i}\big)\;=\;-\,\beta\,r_{t}\,\big(\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{v}_{t}\big)\big(\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}\bm{k}_{i}\big)\,,
(A18)
i.e. interference is proportional to the product of the key
overlap and the value overlap. Orthogonality in either factor suffices
to protect a memory, which is a milder interference profile than the Hebbian
rule (crosstalk from key overlap alone) and than DeltaNet (gated by
key overlap only). The formula is target-independent, since the target
enters only through the residual r t r_{t} . Consequently, writes whose read-out is
already correct ( r t ≈ 0 r_{t}\approx 0 ) cause no interference at all,
regardless of c t c_{t} .
Property A10 (Relaxed Kaczmarz iteration, Fejér monotonicity,
convergence, and overwriting) .
The constraint 𝐯 t ⊤ ​ 𝐒 ​ 𝐤 t = c t \bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{k}_{t}=c_{t} defines the hyperplane
ℋ t = { 𝐒 : ⟨ 𝐯 t ​ 𝐤 t ⊤ , 𝐒 ⟩ F = c t } \mathcal{H}_{t}=\{\bm{S}:{{\left\langle\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top},\bm{S}\right\rangle}}_{\mathrm{F}}=c_{t}\} in ℝ d × d \mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
with Frobenius geometry. With β = β ⋆ \beta=\beta^{\star} the update
( A1 ) is the orthogonal projection onto ℋ t \mathcal{H}_{t} ;
general β \beta gives a relaxed projection. In contrast to a constant
target, consistency of the system { 𝐯 i ⊤ 𝐒 𝐤 i = c i } i \{\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{k}_{i}=c_{i}\}_{i}
is now a genuine condition: it holds if and only if the target vector
( c i ) i (c_{i})_{i} lies in the range of the matrix stacking the rows
𝐚 i ⊤ \bm{a}_{i}^{\top} . In particular, this always holds, whenever the { 𝐚 i } \{\bm{a}_{i}\} are
linearly independent (at most d 2 d^{2} of them). If the system is
consistent, then for every solution 𝐒 ⋆ \bm{S}^{\star} and
any β t ∈ [ 0 , 2 / ‖ 𝐚 t ‖ 2 ] \beta_{t}\in\big[0,\,2/\left\lVert\bm{a}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}\big] ,
‖ 𝑺 t + 1 − 𝑺 ⋆ ‖ F ≤ ‖ 𝑺 t − 𝑺 ⋆ ‖ F (Fejér monotonicity) , \left\lVert\bm{S}_{t+1}-\bm{S}^{\star}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}\;\leq\;\left\lVert\bm{S}_{t}-\bm{S}^{\star}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}\qquad\text{(Fej\'{e}r monotonicity)},
(A19)
and cycling or randomly sampling over the pairs converges linearly to
a solution, at a rate governed by the smallest singular value of the
stacked 𝐚 i ⊤ \bm{a}_{i}^{\top} . From 𝐒 0 = 𝟎 \bm{S}_{0}=\bm{0} the limit is the minimum-Frobenius-norm solution.
The canonical inconsistent case is a repeated pair with a
changed target, ( 𝐤 , 𝐯 , c ) (\bm{k},\bm{v},c) followed later by ( 𝐤 , 𝐯 , c ′ ) (\bm{k},\bm{v},c^{\prime})
with c ′ ≠ c c^{\prime}\neq c : the two hyperplanes are parallel, and the iteration
resolves the conflict by overwriting. The contraction of
Property A6 drives the read-out to the most
recent target, in one shot for β = β ⋆ \beta=\beta^{\star} . In expectation
over a stationary distribution of triples ( 𝐤 , 𝐯 , c ) (\bm{k},\bm{v},c) , the
fixed-point condition is
E ⁡ [ ( 𝒂 ⊤ ​ 𝒔 − c ) ​ 𝒂 ] = 𝟎 , \mathbf{\mathrm{E}}\big[\big(\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{s}-c\big)\bm{a}\big]=\bm{0}\,,
(A20)
the normal equation of the least-squares regression of the target c c
on the feature 𝐚 = 𝐤 ⊗ 𝐯 \bm{a}=\bm{k}\otimes\bm{v} : the memory is an online
linear regressor that learns, in context, to predict the scalar
signal c t c_{t} from the key–value pair.
Property A11 (Capacity: memory can store d 2 d^{2} associations) .
Each triple ( 𝐤 i , 𝐯 i , c i ) (\bm{k}_{i},\bm{v}_{i},c_{i}) contributes one linear constraint on
the d 2 d^{2} parameters of 𝐒 \bm{S} , so up to d 2 d^{2} arbitrary scalars
c i c_{i} can be stored exactly for pairs with linearly independent
{ 𝐤 i ⊗ 𝐯 i } \{\bm{k}_{i}\otimes\bm{v}_{i}\} ( d 2 d^{2} scalar
components). Compare to DeltaNet
with goal 𝐒 ​ 𝐤 i = 𝐯 i \bm{S}\bm{k}_{i}=\bm{v}_{i} that represent d d value vectors.
In an orthonormal design ( { 𝐤 i } \{\bm{k}_{i}\} orthonormal and { 𝐯 i } \{\bm{v}_{i}\}
orthonormal), the minimum-norm solution has the closed form
𝑺 ⋆ = ∑ i c i ​ 𝒗 i ​ 𝒌 i ⊤ , 𝒗 j ⊤ ​ 𝑺 ⋆ ​ 𝒌 i = c i ​ δ i ​ j , \bm{S}^{\star}\;=\;\sum_{i}c_{i}\,\bm{v}_{i}\bm{k}_{i}^{\top}\,,\qquad\bm{v}_{j}^{\top}\bm{S}^{\star}\bm{k}_{i}\;=\;c_{i}\ \delta_{ij}\,,
(A21)
a target-weighted Hebbian superposition realizing the learned
Kronecker delta with per-pair gains. The memory is thus best
understood as a pair-addressed scalar table. It stores a number
c i c_{i} at the address ( 𝐤 i , 𝐯 i ) (\bm{k}_{i},\bm{v}_{i}) , writes by rank-one
updates, reads by the bilinear probe 𝐯 ⊤ ​ 𝐒 ​ 𝐤 \bm{v}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{k} , rewrites by
presenting the same pair with a new target, and deletes via c = 0 c=0
(Property A5 (iii)).
Next, we exemplify the fact
that KronLSTM can store d 2 d^{2} numbers.
Assume d d orthonormal keys { 𝒌 1 , … , 𝒌 d } \{\bm{k}_{1},\ldots,\bm{k}_{d}\} and
d d orthonormal values { 𝒗 1 , … , 𝒗 d } \{\bm{v}_{1},\ldots,\bm{v}_{d}\} .
Then we can build d 2 d^{2} key-value pairs ( 𝒌 i , 𝒗 j ) (\bm{k}_{i},\bm{v}_{j}) with
their associated d 2 d^{2} numbers c i ​ j c_{ij} .
We set β = 1 \beta=1 and start with 𝑺 0 = 𝟎 \bm{S}_{0}=\bm{0} ,
then we iteratively update 𝑺 t \bm{S}_{t} with randomly chosen
i i and j j :
𝑺 t + 1 = 𝑺 t − β ⁡ ( 𝒗 j ⊤ ​ 𝑺 t ​ 𝒌 i − c i ​ j ) ​ 𝒗 j ​ 𝒌 i ⊤ . \bm{S}_{t+1}\;=\;\bm{S}_{t}-\beta\,\big(\bm{v}_{j}^{\top}\,\bm{S}_{t}\,\bm{k}_{i}\,-\,c_{ij}\big)\>\bm{v}_{j}\,\bm{k}_{i}^{\top}\,.
(A22)
If all pairs ( 𝒌 i , 𝒗 j ) (\bm{k}_{i},\bm{v}_{j}) or numbers c i ​ j c_{ij} are seen at least once, the update converged.
If pair ( 𝒌 i , 𝒗 j ) (\bm{k}_{i},\bm{v}_{j}) has already been used in the update rule, then
𝒗 j ⊤ ​ 𝑺 t ​ 𝒌 i = c i ​ j \bm{v}_{j}^{\top}\bm{S}_{t}\bm{k}_{i}=c_{ij} and 𝑺 t + 1 = 𝑺 t \bm{S}_{t+1}=\bm{S}_{t} .
Therefore, if all pairs are seen, 𝑺 t \bm{S}_{t} does not change any more and the update converged.
If pair ( 𝒌 i , 𝒗 j ) (\bm{k}_{i},\bm{v}_{j}) has NOT been stored, then
𝒗 j ⊤ ​ 𝑺 t ​ 𝒌 i = 0 \bm{v}_{j}^{\top}\bm{S}_{t}\bm{k}_{i}=0 and
𝑺 t + 1 = 𝑺 t + c i ​ j ​ 𝒗 j ​ 𝒌 i ⊤ \bm{S}_{t+1}=\bm{S}_{t}+c_{ij}\bm{v}_{j}\bm{k}_{i}^{\top} .
Therefore, if all pairs ( 𝒌 i , 𝒗 j ) (\bm{k}_{i},\bm{v}_{j}) or numbers c i ​ j c_{ij}
are seen at least once, the memory matrix 𝑺 t \bm{S}_{t} is
𝑺 t \displaystyle\bm{S}_{t}\;
= ∑ i = 1 , j = 1 d , d c i ​ j ​ 𝒗 j ​ 𝒌 i ⊤ = 𝑽 ​ 𝑪 ⊤ ​ 𝑲 ⊤ , \displaystyle=\;\sum_{i=1,j=1}^{d,d}c_{ij}\,\bm{v}_{j}\,\bm{k}_{i}^{\top}\;=\;\bm{V}\,\bm{C}^{\top}\,\bm{K}^{\top}\,,
(A23)
with
𝑲 \displaystyle\bm{K}\,
= [ 𝒌 1 , … , 𝒌 d ] ∈ ℝ d × d , 𝑽 = [ 𝒗 1 , … , 𝒗 d ] ∈ ℝ d × d , 𝑪 = ( c i ​ j ) i , j = 1 d ∈ ℝ d × d . \displaystyle=\,\left[\bm{k}_{1},\,\dots,\,\bm{k}_{d}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\,,\quad\bm{V}\,=\,\left[\bm{v}_{1},\,\dots,\,\bm{v}_{d}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\,,\quad\bm{C}\,=\,\left(c_{ij}\right)_{i,j=1}^{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\,.
(A24)
Since 𝒗 j ⊤ ​ 𝑺 t ​ 𝒌 i = c i ​ j \bm{v}_{j}^{\top}\bm{S}_{t}\bm{k}_{i}=c_{ij} , we can now retrieve d 2 d^{2}
stored numbers.
We have
𝑪 ⊤ \displaystyle\bm{C}^{\top}\;
= 𝑽 ⊤ ​ 𝑺 t ​ 𝑲 , \displaystyle=\;\bm{V}^{\top}\,\bm{S}_{t}\,\bm{K}\,,
(A25)
since 𝑲 \bm{K} and 𝑽 \bm{V} are orthogonal. The transpose appears
because the first index of 𝑪 \bm{C} is the key index while the first
index of 𝑺 t \bm{S}_{t} is the value index.
Property A12 (Norm and energy dynamics) .
Using ⟨ 𝐯 t ​ 𝐤 t ⊤ , 𝐒 t ⟩ F = y t = r t + c t {{\left\langle\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top},\bm{S}_{t}\right\rangle}}_{\mathrm{F}}=y_{t}=r_{t}+c_{t} ,
‖ 𝑺 t + 1 ‖ F 2 − ‖ 𝑺 t ‖ F 2 = − 2 ​ β ​ r t ​ ( r t + c t ) + β 2 ​ r t 2 ​ ‖ 𝒌 t ‖ 2 ​ ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ 2 , \left\lVert\bm{S}_{t+1}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}-\left\lVert\bm{S}_{t}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\;=\;-\,2\beta\,r_{t}(r_{t}+c_{t})\;+\;\beta^{2}r_{t}^{2}\,\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}\,\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert^{2},
(A26)
so within the stable range, the state norm stays bounded whenever the
constraint system is consistent. This is a consequence of Fejér
monotonicity: the distance to any fixed solution is non-increasing.
Summary.
The time-varying-target rule ( A1 ) is the most general
member of the Kronecker Delta Rule family: SGD on the scalar quadratic
loss 1 2 ​ ( 𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑺 ​ 𝒌 t − c t ) 2 \tfrac{1}{2}(\bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{k}_{t}-c_{t})^{2} , equivalently
Widrow–Hoff LMS on the feature 𝒂 t = 𝒌 t ⊗ 𝒗 t \bm{a}_{t}=\bm{k}_{t}\otimes\bm{v}_{t} with
desired signal c t c_{t} , equivalently relaxed Kaczmarz on the hyperplanes
⟨ 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ , 𝑺 ⟩ F = c t {{\left\langle\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top},\bm{S}\right\rangle}}_{\mathrm{F}}=c_{t} . The entire analytic
framework is target-independent. That comprises stability range and Newton step
β ⋆ \beta^{\star} , generalized-Householder transition factors with their
parallelization and negative-eigenvalue state-tracking regime, doubly
gated interference, Fejér-monotone linear convergence, d 2 d^{2}
capacity. The targets enter only the driving term, linearly : memories superpose in ( c t ) (c_{t}) , and in an orthonormal
design the memory converges to the target-weighted Hebbian
superposition ∑ i c i ​ 𝒗 i ​ 𝒌 i ⊤ \sum_{i}c_{i}\bm{v}_{i}\bm{k}_{i}^{\top} realizing 𝒗 j ⊤ ​ 𝑺 ​ 𝒌 i = c i ​ δ i ​ j \bm{v}_{j}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{k}_{i}=c_{i}\,\delta_{ij} . Distinguished targets recover
c t ≡ 1 c_{t}\equiv 1 , c t ≡ c c_{t}\equiv c , c t = ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ 2 c_{t}=\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert^{2} and
add two new members: the correlation target c t = 𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝒌 t c_{t}=\bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{k}_{t} ,
for which the identity is a universal fixed point and 𝑺 \bm{S}
parameterizes learned deviations from the standard inner product, and
the erasure target c t = 0 c_{t}=0 , which deletes a single binding in one
Newton step. Functionally, 𝑺 \bm{S} is a pair-addressed scalar table with
write, read, rewrite, and delete operations, therefore it is an editable d 2 d^{2} -slot
scalar memory. In the stochastic view, it is an online in-context
regressor of the signal c t c_{t} on the bilinear feature.
A3.4 Minimally Invasive Memory Revision
The Kronecker Delta Rule is not merely a correction of the
addressed association: at the Newton step it is the smallest
possible one, in every sense in which smallness can reasonably be
asked for. This section elaborates on this topic. Throughout,
𝑺 t ∈ ℝ d × d \bm{S}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d} , the read-out of the pair
( 𝒌 t , 𝒗 t ) (\bm{k}_{t},\bm{v}_{t}) is 𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑺 t ​ 𝒌 t \bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}_{t}\bm{k}_{t} , the target is c t c_{t} , and
r t := 𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑺 t ​ 𝒌 t − c t r_{t}\;:=\;\bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}\,-\,c_{t}
(A27)
is the residual that should be pushed to zero.
A3.4.1 The admissible set
A correction is a matrix 𝑬 ∈ ℝ d × d \bm{E}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d} added to the
memory. It corrects the addressed association if
𝒗 t ⊤ ​ ( 𝑺 t + 𝑬 ) ​ 𝒌 t = c t \bm{v}_{t}^{\top}(\bm{S}_{t}+\bm{E})\bm{k}_{t}=c_{t} . Using
𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑬 ​ 𝒌 t = ⟨ 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ , 𝑬 ⟩ F \bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{E}\bm{k}_{t}={{\left\langle\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top},\bm{E}\right\rangle}}_{\mathrm{F}} , the set of
such corrections is
ℰ t := { 𝑬 ∈ ℝ d × d : ⟨ 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ , 𝑬 ⟩ F = − r t } , \mathcal{E}_{t}\;:=\;\Big\{\bm{E}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\;:\;{{\left\langle\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top},\,\bm{E}\right\rangle}}_{\mathrm{F}}\;=\;-\,r_{t}\Big\}\,,
(A28)
an affine hyperplane in the Hilbert space
( ℝ d × d , ⟨ ⋅ , ⋅ ⟩ F ) (\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},{{\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle}}_{\mathrm{F}}) : it is nonempty, and it
is a proper affine subspace because 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ ≠ 𝟎 \bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}\neq\bm{0}
whenever the pair is nondegenerate. Every statement below compares
members of ℰ t \mathcal{E}_{t} . We hold the requirement fixed
(the addressed association must come out right) and ask which
correction achieves it with the least collateral change. Write
𝑬 ⋆ := − r t ‖ 𝒌 t ‖ 2 ​ ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ 2 ​ 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ = c t − 𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑺 t ​ 𝒌 t ‖ 𝒌 t ‖ 2 ​ ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ 2 ​ 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ , \bm{E}^{\star}\;:=\;-\,\frac{r_{t}}{\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}}\;\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}\;=\;\frac{c_{t}-\bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}}{\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}}\;\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}\,,
(A29)
which is exactly the Kronecker Delta Rule update at the Newton step
β = β ⋆ = 1 / ( ‖ 𝒌 t ‖ 2 ​ ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ 2 ) \beta=\beta^{\star}=1/(\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}) .
A3.4.2 Minimality in norm
Theorem A1 (The Newton step is the smallest correction) .
𝑬 ⋆ ∈ ℰ t \bm{E}^{\star}\in\mathcal{E}_{t} , and for every 𝐄 ∈ ℰ t \bm{E}\in\mathcal{E}_{t}
‖ 𝑬 ‖ F ≥ | r t | ‖ 𝒌 t ‖ ​ ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ = ‖ 𝑬 ⋆ ‖ F , \left\lVert\bm{E}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}\;\;\geq\;\;\frac{{{\left|r_{t}\right|}}}{\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert\,\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert}\;\;=\;\;\left\lVert\bm{E}^{\star}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}\,,
(A30)
with equality if and only if 𝐄 = 𝐄 ⋆ \bm{E}=\bm{E}^{\star} .
Proof.
Admissibility is a computation:
⟨ 𝒗 t 𝒌 t ⊤ , 𝑬 ⋆ ⟩ F = − r t ‖ 𝒗 t 𝒌 t ⊤ ‖ F 2 / ( ‖ 𝒌 t ‖ 2 ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ 2 ) = − r t {{\left\langle\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top},\bm{E}^{\star}\right\rangle}}_{\mathrm{F}}=-r_{t}\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}/(\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert^{2})=-r_{t} , using
‖ 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ ‖ F = ‖ 𝒌 t ‖ ​ ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ \left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}=\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert .
For the bound, apply Cauchy–Schwarz to the constraint in
( A28 ):
| r t | = | ⟨ 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ , 𝑬 ⟩ F | ≤ ‖ 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ ‖ F ​ ‖ 𝑬 ‖ F = ‖ 𝒌 t ‖ ​ ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ ​ ‖ 𝑬 ‖ F , {{\left|r_{t}\right|}}\;=\;{{\left|{{\left\langle\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top},\bm{E}\right\rangle}}_{\mathrm{F}}\right|}}\;\leq\;\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}\,\left\lVert\bm{E}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}\;=\;\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert\,\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert\,\left\lVert\bm{E}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}\,,
(A31)
which is ( A30 ). Cauchy–Schwarz holds with equality
exactly when 𝑬 \bm{E} is a scalar multiple of 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ \bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top} , and the
constraint then pins that scalar to
− r t / ( ‖ 𝒌 t ‖ 2 ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ 2 ) -r_{t}/(\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert^{2}) , i.e. 𝑬 = 𝑬 ⋆ \bm{E}=\bm{E}^{\star} .
∎
Equivalently the geometric view:
𝑺 t + 𝑬 ⋆ \bm{S}_{t}+\bm{E}^{\star} is the orthogonal projection of 𝑺 t \bm{S}_{t} onto the
constraint hyperplane ℋ t = { 𝑺 : 𝒗 t ⊤ ​ 𝑺 ​ 𝒌 t = c t } \mathcal{H}_{t}=\{\bm{S}:\bm{v}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{k}_{t}=c_{t}\} ,
so the Newton step moves the memory along the shortest path from
where it is to where the constraint is satisfied.
Theorem A2 (Minimality in every unitarily invariant norm) .
Let | | | ⋅ | | | {\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\cdot\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|} be any unitarily invariant norm on
ℝ d × d \mathbb{R}^{d\times d} . Then
min 𝑬 ∈ ℰ t ⁡ ‖ | 𝑬 | ‖ = ‖ | 𝑬 ⋆ | ‖ , \min_{\bm{E}\in\mathcal{E}_{t}}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\bm{E}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}\;=\;{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\bm{E}^{\star}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}\,,
(A32)
i.e. the same 𝐄 ⋆ \bm{E}^{\star} is optimal simultaneously for all of
them. In particular, for every Schatten- p p norm,
1 ≤ p ≤ ∞ 1\leq p\leq\infty ,
min 𝑬 ∈ ℰ t ⁡ ‖ 𝑬 ‖ S p = ‖ 𝑬 ⋆ ‖ S p = | r t | ‖ 𝒌 t ‖ ​ ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ , \min_{\bm{E}\in\mathcal{E}_{t}}\left\lVert\bm{E}\right\rVert_{S_{p}}\;=\;\left\lVert\bm{E}^{\star}\right\rVert_{S_{p}}\;=\;\frac{{{\left|r_{t}\right|}}}{\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert\,\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert}\,,
(A33)
the same number for all p p . Therefore, the nuclear, Frobenius and spectral
norms of the optimal correction coincide. The minimizer is unique for
strictly convex norms (Schatten 1 < p < ∞ 1<p<\infty ). For the spectral
and nuclear norms 𝐄 ⋆ \bm{E}^{\star} is a minimizer but need not be the only
one.
Proof.
Let | | | ⋅ | | | ′ {\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\cdot\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{\prime} be the dual norm, so that
| ⟨ 𝑨 , 𝑬 ⟩ F | ≤ ‖ | 𝑨 | ‖ ′ ​ ‖ | 𝑬 | ‖ {{\left|{{\left\langle\bm{A},\bm{E}\right\rangle}}_{\mathrm{F}}\right|}}\leq{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\bm{A}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{\prime}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\bm{E}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|} for all
𝑨 , 𝑬 \bm{A},\bm{E} . Applying this to the constraint gives, for every
𝑬 ∈ ℰ t \bm{E}\in\mathcal{E}_{t} ,
‖ | 𝑬 | ‖ ≥ | r t | ‖ | 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ | ‖ ′ . {\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\bm{E}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}\;\;\geq\;\;\frac{{{\left|r_{t}\right|}}}{{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{\prime}}\,.
(A34)
By von Neumann’s theorem a unitarily invariant norm is a symmetric
gauge function Φ \Phi of the singular values, and the dual norm is
the gauge Φ ′ \Phi^{\prime} of the singular values. The matrix
𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ \bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top} has the single nonzero singular value
‖ 𝒌 t ‖ ​ ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ \left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert , so
‖ | 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ | ‖ = ‖ 𝒌 t ‖ ​ ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ ​ Φ ​ ( 𝒆 1 ) , ‖ | 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ | ‖ ′ = ‖ 𝒌 t ‖ ​ ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ ​ Φ ′ ​ ( 𝒆 1 ) . {\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}=\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert\,\Phi(\bm{e}_{1})\,,\qquad{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{\prime}=\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert\,\Phi^{\prime}(\bm{e}_{1})\,.
(A35)
Now Φ ⁡ ( 𝒆 1 ) ​ Φ ′ ​ ( 𝒆 1 ) = 1 \Phi(\bm{e}_{1})\Phi^{\prime}(\bm{e}_{1})=1 : indeed
Φ ′ ​ ( 𝒆 1 ) = sup { x 1 : Φ ⁡ ( 𝒙 ) ≤ 1 } \Phi^{\prime}(\bm{e}_{1})=\sup\{x_{1}:\Phi(\bm{x})\leq 1\} , and since a symmetric
gauge is nondecreasing in each | x i | {{\left|x_{i}\right|}} , replacing 𝒙 \bm{x} by
( x 1 , 0 , … , 0 ) (x_{1},0,\dots,0) does not increase Φ \Phi , so the supremum is
attained on the first axis and equals 1 / Φ ⁡ ( 𝒆 1 ) 1/\Phi(\bm{e}_{1}) . Since
𝑬 ⋆ \bm{E}^{\star} is rank one with singular value
| r t | / ( ‖ 𝒌 t ‖ ​ ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ ) {{\left|r_{t}\right|}}/(\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert) ,
‖ | 𝑬 ⋆ | ‖ = | r t | ‖ 𝒌 t ‖ ​ ‖ 𝒗 t ‖ ​ Φ ​ ( 𝒆 1 ) = | r t | ‖ | 𝒗 t ​ 𝒌 t ⊤ | ‖ ′ , {\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\bm{E}^{\star}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}\;=\;\frac{{{\left|r_{t}\right|}}}{\left\lVert\bm{k}_{t}\right\rVert\left\lVert\bm{v}_{t}\right\rVert}\,\Phi(\bm{e}_{1})\;=\;\frac{{{\left|r_{t}\right|}}}{{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\bm{v}_{t}\bm{k}_{t}^{\top}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{\prime}}\,,
(A36)
which is exactly the lower bound ( A34 ): 𝑬 ⋆ \bm{E}^{\star}
attains it. The Schatten case follows from Φ p ​ ( 𝒆 1 ) = 1 \Phi_{p}(\bm{e}_{1})=1 for
every p p . Uniqueness for a strictly convex norm holds because
ℰ t \mathcal{E}_{t} is convex and a strictly convex function has at most one
minimizer on a convex set; for the spectral norm uniqueness fails
already at d = 2 d=2 with 𝒌 = 𝒗 = 𝒆 1 \bm{k}=\bm{v}=\bm{e}_{1} and r t = − 1 r_{t}=-1 , where
𝑬 = diag ⁡ ( 1 , δ ) \bm{E}=\operatorname{diag}(1,\delta) is admissible and has spectral norm 1 1 for
every | δ | ≤ 1 {{\left|\delta\right|}}\leq 1 .
∎
A3.4.3 Minimality of support: the correction is surgical
Norm-optimality says the correction is small. The next theorem has a stronger statement even: it is the only correction
that stays inside the addressed directions at all. No optimization is
involved.
Theorem A3 (Unique correction with addressed support) .
𝑬 ⋆ \bm{E}^{\star} is the unique 𝐄 ∈ ℰ t \bm{E}\in\mathcal{E}_{t} whose column space is
contained in span ⁡ { 𝐯 t } \spanop\{\bm{v}_{t}\} and whose row space is contained in
span ⁡ { 𝐤 t } \spanop\{\bm{k}_{t}\} .
Proof.
